People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I wasn’t sure if that’s something worth coming in for, but it’s been bothering me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you for seeing me. I mean, it’s probably not a huge thing, but… what pushed me to come in is I’ve been getting really jumpy. Like if a door slams, or someone rolls a suitcase fast across the lobby at work, I jump like it’s happening again, and then it takes me a minute to get my heart to settle down. I wasn’t sure if that’s something worth coming in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: When you jump in response to a sudden noise, is it because it reminds you of something specific?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when a door slams or someone wheels a suitcase really fast across the lobby, my body just reacts like it’s happening again. I jump before I can even think, and then it takes me a minute to get my heart back down. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is the 'happening again' you mentioned related to something traumatic that occurred in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic exactly, but something happened at work with a guest leaning over the counter and knocking equipment toward me. And now, out of nowhere, I’ll get this picture of it in my head, even when I’m just trying to answer a normal call. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because nothing’s happening in that moment, but it just pops up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and flashbacks?\n\nPatient: Um… about two weeks, I think. Since that guest threatened me at the front desk. I kept thinking it would just settle down on its own, so I didn’t want to overreact, but it’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still going to work and everything, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to be there. But I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, just to avoid walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened. When I go by it, my chest tightens up and I just kind of want to get away from it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway. I mean, I’ve been a little more on edge, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking badly about people or, like, blaming myself. At least not that I can think of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, maybe I’ve had a night or two where it took me a little longer to settle, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real trouble sleeping. I’m still getting through the night okay, I think. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by things that wouldn't have bothered you before the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, that’s probably the thing that made me feel like I should come in. I don’t want to overstate it, but if a door slams or someone pulls one of those rolling suitcases quickly across the lobby, I kind of jump like it’s happening again. My heart starts racing, and it takes me a minute to calm back down. Is that… the kind of startle reaction you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much of an effort is it for you to go to work and be in the areas where the incident happened?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little harder than I want to admit, I guess. I’m still going in and doing my job, so it’s not like I’m refusing or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can. Walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten, and I just kind of want to get away from it fast. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding it, exactly, but… yeah, it takes some effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the incident at work with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I haven’t been trying to make a big thing of it with friends or family. I usually just say I’m okay or change the subject. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this experience at work affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job duties?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I’m still showing up, and I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything. I know that’s not ideal. I’m trying not to make it anyone else’s problem, but it probably has affected how smoothly I’m doing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because they remind you of the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Not really social events, exactly… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people so much as that area at work. Like I said, I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past the spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it.  \n\nSo… I guess it’s more avoiding the place than avoiding social stuff, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I kept thinking it would just settle down on its own, so I didn’t want to overreact, but it’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still going to work and everything, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to be there. But I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, just to avoid walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened. When I go by it, my chest tightens up and I just kind of want to get away from it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway. I mean, I’ve been a little more on edge, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking badly about people or, like, blaming myself. At least not that I can think of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, maybe I’ve had a night or two where it took me a little longer to settle, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real trouble sleeping. I’m still getting through the night okay, I think. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by things that wouldn't have bothered you before the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, that’s probably the thing that made me feel like I should come in. I don’t want to overstate it, but if a door slams or someone pulls one of those rolling suitcases quickly across the lobby, I kind of jump like it’s happening again. My heart starts racing, and it takes me a minute to calm back down. Is that… the kind of startle reaction you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much of an effort is it for you to go to work and be in the areas where the incident happened?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little harder than I want to admit, I guess. I’m still going in and doing my job, so it’s not like I’m refusing or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can. Walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten, and I just kind of want to get away from it fast. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding it, exactly, but… yeah, it takes some effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the incident at work with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I haven’t been trying to make a big thing of it with friends or family. I usually just say I’m okay or change the subject. It’s easier not to get into it, I guess… kind of like at work, I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can because walking past that spot in the lobby makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this experience at work affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job duties?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I’m still showing up, and I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything. But I guess my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk.\n\nAnd I keep… finding reasons not to cross the lobby, even when guests need help over there. I know that’s not ideal. I’m trying not to make it anyone else’s problem, but it probably has affected how smoothly I’m doing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because they remind you of the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Not really social events, exactly… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people so much as that area at work. Like I said, I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past the spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it.  \n\nSo… I guess it’s more avoiding the place than avoiding social stuff, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding the front desk area at work altogether since the incident?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no… I don’t want to make it sound like I just refuse to go there. But I do avoid it when I can. I’ve been taking the back hallway a lot because if I walk past that spot in the lobby where it happened, my chest tightens and I just kind of want to get away from it.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal at work… but I’m trying to manage it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when a door slams or someone wheels a suitcase really fast across the lobby, my body just reacts like it’s happening again. I jump before I can even think, and then it takes me a minute to get my heart back down. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is the 'happening again' you mentioned related to something traumatic that occurred in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic exactly, but something happened at work with a guest leaning over the counter and knocking equipment toward me. And now, out of nowhere, I’ll get this picture of it in my head, even when I’m just trying to answer a normal call. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because nothing’s happening in that moment, but it just pops up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and flashbacks?\n\nPatient: Um… about two weeks, I think. Since that guest threatened me at the front desk. I kept thinking it would just settle down on its own, so I didn’t want to overreact, but it’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still going to work and everything, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to be there. But I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, just to avoid walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened. When I go by it, my chest tightens up and I just kind of want to get away from it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway. I mean, I’ve been a little more on edge, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking badly about people or, like, blaming myself. At least not that I can think of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, maybe I’ve had a night or two where it took me a little longer to settle, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real trouble sleeping. I’m still getting through the night okay, I think. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by things that wouldn't have bothered you before the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, that’s probably the thing that made me feel like I should come in. I don’t want to overstate it, but if a door slams or someone pulls one of those rolling suitcases quickly across the lobby, I kind of jump like it’s happening again. My heart starts racing, and it takes me a minute to calm back down. Is that… the kind of startle reaction you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much of an effort is it for you to go to work and be in the areas where the incident happened?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little harder than I want to admit, I guess. I’m still going in and doing my job, so it’s not like I’m refusing or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can. Walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten, and I just kind of want to get away from it fast. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding it, exactly, but… yeah, it takes some effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the incident at work with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I haven’t been trying to make a big thing of it with friends or family. I usually just say I’m okay or change the subject. It’s easier not to get into it, I guess… kind of like at work, I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can because walking past that spot in the lobby makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this experience at work affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job duties?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I’m still showing up, and I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything. But I guess my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk.\n\nAnd I keep… finding reasons not to cross the lobby, even when guests need help over there. I know that’s not ideal. I’m trying not to make it anyone else’s problem, but it probably has affected how smoothly I’m doing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because they remind you of the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Not really social events, exactly… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people so much as that area at work. Like I said, I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past the spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it.  \n\nSo… I guess it’s more avoiding the place than avoiding social stuff, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding the front desk area at work altogether since the incident?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no… I don’t want to make it sound like I just refuse to go there. But I do avoid it when I can. I’ve been taking the back hallway a lot because if I walk past that spot in the lobby where it happened, my chest tightens and I just kind of want to get away from it.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal at work… but I’m trying to manage it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding the front desk area at work is causing significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s, like, ruining my life or anything… I’m still going in and doing my best. But yeah, it has caused some problems.\n\nMy manager shortened a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk, and I keep catching myself making excuses not to cross the lobby when guests need help over there. I know that’s not great. I’m trying to handle it, but… yeah, it’s affecting work more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding the front desk area at work is causing problems in your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I don’t think my coworkers are mad at me or anything. At least I hope not. But I can tell it’s probably making things harder for them.\n\nI’m still showing up and trying to do my job, but because I’m slower at the reservation desk, my manager shortened a couple of my shifts. And I keep finding reasons not to cross the lobby when guests need help, so I’m sure someone else ends up picking that up. I don’t want to be a problem for the team, but… yeah, I guess it is affecting things some.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway. I mean, I’ve been a little more on edge, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking badly about people or, like, blaming myself. At least not that I can think of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, maybe I’ve had a night or two where it took me a little longer to settle, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real trouble sleeping. I’m still getting through the night okay, I think. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by things that wouldn't have bothered you before the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, that’s probably the thing that made me feel like I should come in. I don’t want to overstate it, but if a door slams or someone pulls one of those rolling suitcases quickly across the lobby, I kind of jump like it’s happening again. My heart starts racing, and it takes me a minute to calm back down. Is that… the kind of startle reaction you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much of an effort is it for you to go to work and be in the areas where the incident happened?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little harder than I want to admit, I guess. I’m still going in and doing my job, so it’s not like I’m refusing or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can. Walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten, and I just kind of want to get away from it fast. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding it, exactly, but… yeah, it takes some effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the incident at work with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I haven’t been trying to make a big thing of it with friends or family. I usually just say I’m okay or change the subject. It’s easier not to get into it, I guess… kind of like at work, I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can because walking past that spot in the lobby makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this experience at work affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job duties?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I’m still showing up, and I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything. But I guess my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk.\n\nAnd I keep… finding reasons not to cross the lobby, even when guests need help over there. I know that’s not ideal. I’m trying not to make it anyone else’s problem, but it probably has affected how smoothly I’m doing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because they remind you of the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Not really social events, exactly… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people so much as that area at work. Like I said, I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past the spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it.  \n\nSo… I guess it’s more avoiding the place than avoiding social stuff, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding the front desk area at work altogether since the incident?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no… I don’t want to make it sound like I just refuse to go there. But I do avoid it when I can. I’ve been taking the back hallway a lot because if I walk past that spot in the lobby where it happened, my chest tightens and I just kind of want to get away from it.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal at work… but I’m trying to manage it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding the front desk area at work is causing significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s, like, ruining my life or anything… I’m still going in and doing my best. But yeah, it has caused some problems.\n\nMy manager shortened a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk, and I keep catching myself making excuses not to cross the lobby when guests need help over there. I know that’s not great. I’m trying to handle it, but… yeah, it’s affecting work more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding the front desk area at work is causing problems in your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I don’t think my coworkers are mad at me or anything. At least I hope not. But I can tell it’s probably making things harder for them.\n\nI’m still showing up and trying to do my job, but because I’m slower at the reservation desk, my manager shortened a couple of my shifts. And I keep finding reasons not to cross the lobby when guests need help, so I’m sure someone else ends up picking that up. I don’t want to be a problem for the team, but… yeah, I guess it is affecting things some.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the front desk area at work?\n\nPatient: About two weeks now… pretty much since that guest threatened me at the front desk. I mean, I’m still going in, so I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s when I started taking the long way around and trying not to be near that area if I can help it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by things that wouldn't have bothered you before the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, that’s probably the thing that made me feel like I should come in. My heart starts racing, and it takes me a minute to calm back down. I jump before I can even think, and then it takes me a minute to get my heart back down. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is the 'happening again' you mentioned related to something traumatic that occurred in your past?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I don’t know if I’d call it traumatic exactly, but something happened at work with a guest leaning over the counter and knocking equipment toward me. And now, out of nowhere, I’ll get this picture of it in my head, even when I’m just trying to answer a normal call. It’s kind of embarrassing, honestly, because nothing’s happening in that moment, but it just pops up.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these jumpy reactions and flashbacks?\n\nPatient: Um… about two weeks, I think. Since that guest threatened me at the front desk. I kept thinking it would just settle down on its own, so I didn’t want to overreact, but it’s been pretty consistent since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I’m still going to work and everything, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to be there. But I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, just to avoid walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened. When I go by it, my chest tightens up and I just kind of want to get away from it. Is that what you mean by avoiding?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a big way, anyway. I mean, I’ve been a little more on edge, but I haven’t really noticed myself thinking badly about people or, like, blaming myself. At least not that I can think of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, maybe I’ve had a night or two where it took me a little longer to settle, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real trouble sleeping. I’m still getting through the night okay, I think. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by things that wouldn't have bothered you before the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, that’s probably the thing that made me feel like I should come in. I don’t want to overstate it, but if a door slams or someone pulls one of those rolling suitcases quickly across the lobby, I kind of jump like it’s happening again. My heart starts racing, and it takes me a minute to calm back down. Is that… the kind of startle reaction you mean?\n\nDoctor: How much of an effort is it for you to go to work and be in the areas where the incident happened?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little harder than I want to admit, I guess. I’m still going in and doing my job, so it’s not like I’m refusing or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can. Walking past that spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten, and I just kind of want to get away from it fast. I don’t know if that counts as avoiding it, exactly, but… yeah, it takes some effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the incident at work with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I haven’t been trying to make a big thing of it with friends or family. I usually just say I’m okay or change the subject. It’s easier not to get into it, I guess… kind of like at work, I’ve been taking the back hallway when I can because walking past that spot in the lobby makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Has this experience at work affected your relationships with coworkers or your ability to perform your job duties?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I’m still showing up, and I don’t think I’m doing terribly or anything. But I guess my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk.\n\nAnd I keep… finding reasons not to cross the lobby, even when guests need help over there. I know that’s not ideal. I’m trying not to make it anyone else’s problem, but it probably has affected how smoothly I’m doing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social activities or events because they remind you of the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Not really social events, exactly… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding people so much as that area at work. Like I said, I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can, because walking past the spot in the lobby where it happened makes my chest tighten and I just want to get away from it.  \n\nSo… I guess it’s more avoiding the place than avoiding social stuff, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding the front desk area at work altogether since the incident?\n\nPatient: Not altogether, no… I don’t want to make it sound like I just refuse to go there. But I do avoid it when I can. I’ve been taking the back hallway a lot because if I walk past that spot in the lobby where it happened, my chest tightens and I just kind of want to get away from it.\n\nI know that probably isn’t ideal at work… but I’m trying to manage it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding the front desk area at work is causing significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s, like, ruining my life or anything… I’m still going in and doing my best. But yeah, it has caused some problems.\n\nMy manager shortened a couple of my shifts because I’ve been slower at the reservation desk, and I keep catching myself making excuses not to cross the lobby when guests need help over there. I know that’s not great. I’m trying to handle it, but… yeah, it’s affecting work more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding the front desk area at work is causing problems in your relationships with your coworkers?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I mean, I don’t think my coworkers are mad at me or anything. At least I hope not. But I can tell it’s probably making things harder for them.\n\nI’m still showing up and trying to do my job, but because I’m slower at the reservation desk, my manager shortened a couple of my shifts. I don’t want to be a problem for the team, but… yeah, I guess it is affecting things some.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the front desk area at work?\n\nPatient: About two weeks now… pretty much since that guest threatened me at the front desk.
